October 24, 1945
In replying please reter to 1T11:1700
In re: Tokyo Electric Light Company, Limited First Mortgage Gold Bonds 6% Do
Series due June 15, 1953
War Department
Washington, D. C.
Gentlemen:
As Fiscal Agent for the above issue, we have received, since cessation of
hostilities in Japan, a number of inquiries from bondholders as to the present
status of these bonds.
Our records indicate that there are $47,393,000. of Dollar Series Bonds
of this issue outstanding, of which about $27,000,O00. of bonds are held in Japan,
leaving over $20,000,000. of bonds held, for the most part, in this country. In
1939 at the time of the nationalization of the Japanese Power and Light Industry
part of the property of Tokyo Electric Light Company, Limited was taken over by
Japan Electric Generation and Transmission Company Limited and at the same time the
Japanese Government guaranteed principal of and interest on the bonds of this issuer
Under present regulations we are, of course, unable to write to and
obtain from either Tokyo Electric Light Company, Limited or The Mitsui Bank, Limited
Trustee of the issue, information as to the extent of damage to the power plants,
distribution and transmission lines, sub-stations and other property subject to the
Mortgage and concerning the financial condition of Tokyo Electric Light Company,
Limited.
In order that we may be in a position to answer bondholders' inquiries,
we are wondering if it would be possible for you to obtain, through the United
States military authorities in Japan, information of the character above mentioned
from the Mitsui Bank, Limited, as Trustee, or from Tokyo Electric Light Company,
Limited. If this could be done, it would be very much to the interest of American
holders as without up-to-date information it is difficult for them to evaluate theii
bonds.
We might mention that in December, 194l we made available to Lt. Col.
Frederick D. Sharp, G.S.C. numerous maps, blueprints of power plants, descriptions
of generating stations, photographs, etc. for the use of the military authorities
and additional material was loaned to the Military Intelli/ence Division, G2, on
several occasions thereafter, all with the object of coorerating in the war effort.
Yours veytruly
Arthuy
Bur
AML
Vibe President

the property of Toho Electric Power Company, Limited, was taken over by Japan Elee-

To the Officer in Charge of the American Mission,
Managua.
The Secretary of State encloses a copy of a
letter dated November 15, l945, from Colonel Irving A.
Lindberg, Collector-General of Customs and Member
of the Commission to Regulate Trade, Republic of
Nicaragua, inquiring whether steps have been taken
to protect American investors who purchased Japanese
dollar bonds.
Colonel Lindberg's letter does not contain
sufficient information to enable the Department to
reply satisfactorily to his inquiry. Unless the
Embassy perceives objection, it is requested that the
Embassy direct a letter to Coñonel Lindberg, sugges-
ting that he furnish to the Department the pertinent
facts as to the identification and description of
the bonds, and the manner and date of their acqui-
sition by him or the present holder thereof.
Upon receipt of this information, the Department
will be glad to give further consideration to Colonel
Lindberg's inquiry.

In reply refer to
FE
My dear Dr. Canby:
Reference is made to your recent letter (Jammary,
1946) relative to the 5 percent sterling bonds of the
Japanese Government, issue of l907, held by you.
As you probably know, there was established in
London many years ago the Corporation of Forsign Bend-
holders to represent the interests of holders of sterling
bonds originally floated in the London market. The
Corporation operates under an Act of Parliament and
enjoys a semi-official position in matters relative to
foreign Government obligations payable in sterling. The
great bulk of such obligations are usually held by
British subjects and those held by persons of other
nationalities are generally accorded approximately the
same treatment as those held by British subjeets.
The Corporation of Foreign Bondholders has its head-
quarters at i7 Moorgate, London, England. You may wish
to write to the Corporation regarding the bonds held by
you.

issued aside from interest and sinking fund payments which have been in

upon the grounds, among other things, that The Mitsui Bank Limited is un-

us that they cannot state what if any action the Court may take on this
matter. Further, such counsel advise that they doubt the efficacy of any
order the Court might enter with regard to a trust indenture executed in
Japan and relating to a trust res there situated.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
WASHINGTON
In reply refer to
FN
My dear Mr. Reck:
Reference is made to your 1otter of August 5, 1946,
relative to two loo0 Imperial Japanese Government
6-1/2% bonds due in 1954, one 500 Imperia1 Japanese
Government 6-1/2% bond due in/1954, and one $1000
German Government 5-1/2% bond due in 1965 which form part
of the estate of Frank M. Stenger.
Apparently the article in the press referred to by
you was based on a Press Release by the Department dated
August 1, 1946. This release was intended to refer to
equity interests in Japanese business enterprises rather
than to Japanese Government bonds.
A definitive statement regarding the future posi-
tion of Japanese Government dollar bonds or German
Government dollar bonds is not now possible, but the
following may be helpful to your understanding of the
Department's policy with reference to such obligations.
The Department considers that investment in foreign
bonds is primarily a private transaction into which the
purchaser voluntarily enters after calculating the risks
involved. In case of default on dollar bonds issued by
a foreign government, it is not the practice of the
Department to negotiate directly for American holders,
but it does endeavor to render such assistance as it
appropriately can to them or their representatives. In
this connection your attention may be called to the
8-546
Foreign Bondholders Protective Council, Incorporated, 90
Broad Street, New York, New York, which was established
several years ago to serve as a central representative
body. The Council was organized with the encouragement
of the Department, but it acts on its own responsibility.
Ct..
It is understood that the Council is now interesting
itself in such obligations as those mentioned by you and
you
Mr. B. Harry Reck,
Reck & Claus,
Mendota, Illinois.

=== Page 244 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
you'may wish to write to it in this connection.
It will be appreciated that the Department cannot
state at the present time what provisions, if any, may
be inserted in the peace treaties rogarding such obliga-
tions, or what provision for prior payment of claims
arising from the war may be made. You are doubtless
aware that there will be many claims and demands against
the Japanese and German Governments and against persons
and municipalities in those countries, of which such
bonds as those referred to by you will probably constitute
only a small part.
Nevertheless, the Department will make a record, for
such consideration as may be appropriate, of any infor-
mation you may send to it regarding the bonds mentioned
by you. Such information should include, in addition
to that contained in your letter of August 5, 1946, the
manner and date of the acquisition of the bonds by Mr.
Stenger, the purchase price, the last payment of interest
thereon, and the manner (whether by birth or naturali-
zation) and date of acquisition of American citizenship
by the heirs.
If this Government should take any action with
respect to such obligations, due announcement will be
made in the press in order that interested American
citizens may be properly informed.
Sincerely yours,
For the Secretary of State:
James C. Corliss, Assistant Chief
Division of Finan cial Affairs

My dear Miss Reed:
M
Reference is made to your letter of August 7, 1946,

8
father, Mr. John P. Whaley, Box 605, Bay View, Michigan.
Apparently the article in the New York paper men-
tioned by you was based on a Press Release by the
Department dated August 1, 1946. This Release was
intended to refer to equity interests in Japanese
companies rather than to bonds, mortgages and similar
obligations that would ordinarily evidence liens on
assets, earnings or other property of such companies.
A definitive statement regarding the future position
of Tokyo Electric Light and Fower Company dollar bonds
is not now possible, but the following may be helpful to
your understanding of the Department's policy with
reference to such obligations.
In general, the Department considers that invest-
ment in foreign bonds is essentially a private trans-
action into which the purchaser voluntarily enters after
calculatin the risk involved. In the case of obliga-
tions issued by private entities, it is assumed that
holders of such obligations will avai l themselves of the
usual methods for enforcement of the contractual terms
when resort to s uch measures becomes practicable.
In the case of dollar bonds issued or guaranteed
by a Government, while it is not the practice of the
Department to negotiate directly for American holders,
An
if default should occur, the Department does endeavor
to render such assistance as it appropriately can to
them or their representatives. In this connection
your attention may be called to the Foreign
Bondholders

agreed upon. As you are no doubt aware, there will be
many claims and demands of different types against Japan

My dear Mr. Pratt:
BONDHOLDERS/8-2346
Reference is made to your letter of August 25,
1946, regarding the Seven Percent First Mortmasge Bands
of the Ujigawa Electric Power Company, Limited, due
March 15, 1945. You inquire as to the stops you
might take in the interests of the bondhelders.
A definitive reply to your inquiry is not now
possible, but the following may be helpful te your
understanding of the Department's positien relative
to such obligations.
In general, the Depertment considere that investmemt
in foreign bonds is essentially a private transaction
into which the purchaser voluntarily enters after ealem-
lating the risk involved. In the case of obligatiems
issued by private entities, it is assumed that holders
of such obligations will be prepared to avail themselves.
if necessary, of the usual methods for enforeement of
the contractual terms when resort to such measures
becomes practicable.
In case of default upon obligations guaranteed by
a Government, while it is not the practice of the Depart-
ment to negotiate directly for American holders, the
Department does endeavor to render such assistanee as
it appropriately can to them or their representatives.
In this connection attention may be called to the
Foreign Bondholders Protective Council, Incorperated,
90 Broad Street, New York, New York, organised several

years

release was intended to be bread enough te inelude

the American interests that might be affested by the

Department's invitation to American helders ef equity
interests in Japanese oorporations still holds good.
Investment in foreign seourities is considered
by the Department as primarily a private transaction
into which the purchaser voluntarily enters after
caloulation of the risks involved. In the ease of
equity securities and bonds of private orgamizations
not guaranteed by a Japanese Governmental entity, it
is assumed that holders will be prepared to avail
themselves of the usual methods to enforee their
rights, when resort to such measures is or becomes
possible. Since January 2, 1947, as you my know,
it has been possible for Amerioans residing in the
United States to communicate directly with Japan to
secure information regarding their interests in that
country. Possibly you or your clients may wish to
take advantage of this relaxation of the restrietions
upon communication.
In the case of default of bonds issued or guar-
anteed by a governmental entity, while the Department
does not negotiate directly for American holders, it
does endeavor to render such assistance as it appro-
priately can to them or their representatives. In
this connection you are probably familiar with the
Foreign Bondholders Proteotive Council, 90 Broad Street,
New York, New York, whioh was organized several years
ago to serve as a central representative body. The
Council was established with the encouragement of the
Department, but operates on its own responsibility.

Department transmitted to its representative at Tokye
a complete statement of the dollar bonds issued or
guaranteed by Japanese governmental entities. This
statement was forwarded for the information and use
of the United States Political Adviser and of the

after caloulating the risk involved. In the case of
obligations issued by private entities, it is assumed

ago as a central representative body. Although the

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Dear Sir:
The Guaranty Trust Company of New York has informed me that your
department has expressed itself to them that you will be glad to make a
record, for such consideration as may be appropriate, of any information
relative to residents in the United States who hold bonds of Japanese
corporations.
Mr. Kichitaro Mutow has to my knowledge been a resident and an
energetic and reliable 'business man in Los Angeles for upwards of fifteen
to twenty years, prior to December 7, 194l. During that period he invested
his savings in the purchase of various japanese utility bonds by reason of
the high rate of interest paid and the low rate at which the bonds were
hold.
During the war Mr. Mutow was, of course, evacuated. He has now
returned to Los Angeles and is desirous of doing that which he can towards
recovering something upon his investments. The Guaranty Trust Company
likewise stated in their letter that they had been informed by your office
that it might be found appropriate for the United States Government to take
some action with respect to these bonds and to accept proofs of claim filed.
I am unaware, of course, as to the position your department takes
or as to the condition of affairs in Japan. I have understood that in 1939
894.511
there was a nationalization of the Japanese Power and Light Industry under
which part of the properties upon which the bonds were issued were taken
over by a government corporation and the Japanese Government guaranteed
certain of the bonds. During the war I understand that extensive transfers
of these utility properties were made. I have understood that especially
the properties of the Tokio Electric Light Co. were in some manner conveyb
to a concern known as the Kanto Electric Supply Company. Your office
DR D i
undoubtedly has more or less accurate information upon these subjects. 
I am enclosing herewith a record of the bonds held by Mr. Mutow and
S
request that you give it your attention. If there are any proofs of claim
which you desire to have filed, Mr. Mutow will so do. If it is desired to
have any more definite proof of the ownership of the bonds, Mr. Mutow is in
position so that he can very well do so as his records are in good shapes.
Please advise at your early convenience.
Bev.
chm
Diast.
Yours raspectfully,

My dear Mr. Aoki:

dollar, sterling, and frano bonds. You ask partioularly

A definitive reply to your inquiry is not now
possible, but the following may be helpful to your
understanding of the general situation and of the
Department's policy with respect to such obligations.
You will understand that these comments refer to bonds
held by American citizens.
The Department considers that investment in foreign
bonds is primarily a private transaction into whioh the
purchaser enters after calculation of the risks involved.
In case of default on dollar bonds issued by private
entities, it is assumed that the holders dil be
prepared, if necessary, to avail themselves of the
usual methods to exercise any rights they may possess,
whenever and wherever resort to such measures is
possible. In case of default on dollar bonds ismued
by or guaranteed by government al entities, it is not
CSA

for American holders, but it does endeawr to render
such assistance as it properly can to such holders or
their representatives. In this conection attontion

To the
American Consular Orficer in Chargo,
Alexandria, Egypt.
The Acting Secretary of State refers to the
Conaulate's despatch number 30l, dated February 14
1947, enclosing a copy of a letter dated February 12,
1947, from Mr. Aubroy L. Molossi, Assistant Manager
of the Ford Motor Company, Alexandria, Egypt, regarding
steps taken to protect the interest of American helders
of the City of Tokyo Five Per Cent Sterling Bonds of
1912.
There is attached to the present instruction the
text of a letter which the Consulate may use in
replying to Mr. Molossi.
For the information of the Consulate there is
also enclosed a specimen letter which the Department
might use in replying to a similar inquiry from an
American holder of City of Tokyo dollar bonds. It
will be noted that in the case of dollar bonds, the
lead would be taken by the Foreign Bondholders
Protective Council, while the interests of sterling
holders would be taken care of by the corresponding
British body, the Council of Foreign Bondholders.
The two Councils work together quite closely, and
they have agreed that any arrangement made by either
of them will apply to ali bonds involved, regardless
of the nationality of the holders. Thus any adjustment
CS/V
that may apply to the City of Tokyo Five Pereent bonds
held by British subjects would apply also to the bonds
held by Mr. Molossi.

The Department considers that investment in
foreign bonds is primarily a private transaetion inte
which the purchaser voluntarily enters after ealeulatisn
of the risks involved. In the case of default upen
obligations issued or guaranteed by foreign governmental
entities, it is not the practice of the Department to
negotiate directly for American holders, but it dees
endeavor to render such assistance as it appropriately
can to them or to their representatives. When such
obligations are payable in currencies other than
dollars, however, the Department is rarely able to de
more than endeavor to have bonds of a given issue
held by American citizens accorded treatment no less
favorable than that accorded to bonds held by natienals
of other countries, including nationals of the country
in whose currency the obligations are payable, by whem
the majority of the bonds would usually be held.
As you probably know, the Council of Foreign
Bondholders, Limited, was established in Great Britain
many years ago to protect the interests of holders of
bonds of foreign governments floated in the British
market. The Council operates as a semi-official bedy,
under an Act of Parliament, and endeavors especially
to safeguard the interests of holders of sterling
bonds in default. The Department understands that it
is the policy of the Counoil to have any adjustment
of default of bonds of any given sterling issue apply
to all bonds of that issue, regardless of the natienality
of the holders. You may wish to write to the Couneil
at 6-7 King William Street, London, East Center 4, and
ask it to keep you informed of any developments that
might affect the position of the bonds held by you.
Neanwhile, the Department will be glad to make a
record of your holding, for such consideration as may
be found to be appropriate.

February 13, 1947
Honorable John L. McMillan, M.C.
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. McMillan:
I know that you are not a legal adviser to your con-
stituents but I am in a quandry about a matter and I think perhaps
you can put me on the track of an answer at least.
We represent an estate which owns two Japanese bonds -
one of the City of Tokyo and the other of a utility corporation in
Japan. At the beginning of the War we had to register all such
bonds with some branch of the Government and did so. That was
before the death of the party owning them, and I know that trading
in such securities was forbidden, probably by a proclamation of
the Fresident or of some agency below him, at least to the public
exchanges.
I heve not been able to locate in my library any of the
proclamations so as to know the extent of the bar. The administra-
tor wants to wind up the estate and what to do with these bonds is
a question. If you could advise me to what branch of the Government
to apply for information, I would be very much obliged. You doubt-
less have kept up with these various ar measures. I have no idea
that the bonds are worth anything, but they are part of the estate
and so have to be dealt with. Any suggestions that you can make I
will be very happy to receive.
Thanking you, I am
Yours very truly,
STEVENSON & LINDSAY

Dear Miss Shutack:
This will aoknowledge your letter of Maroh 4, 1947,
in which you request information with referenoe to filing a
claim with this Office for a Shinyetsu Electric Power.Compamy
bond.
Under the provisions of the Trading with the memy Aot,
as amended, the Offioe of Alien Property entertains debt oleims,
i.e., claims asserting a debt or other obligation, only against
persons or enterprises whose property it has vested or over which
it otherwise has acquired jurisdiction. The records of this Offiee
do not disclose that it has aoquired any property of the Shinyetsu
Electric Power Company. Accordingly, it cannot entertain any
claim against that enterprise.
Since the informmtion you seek appears also to relate to
matters within the jurisdiction of the Department of State, a
copy of your letter is being referred to that Department for
consideration and further reply.

First Mortgage 7% Sinking Fund Gold Bonds
Series A, due August 1, 1944
First and General Mortgage 6-1/2% Sinking
Fund Gold Bonds, due July 1, 1950
Shinyetsu Denryoku Kabushiki Kaisha
(Shinyetsu Electric Power Company, Limited)
First Mortgage 6-1/2% Sinking Fund Bonda
due December 1, 1952
At the outbreak of the war all outstanding Bonds of the
above listed issues were secured by mortgages on plants and other
properties of the respective obligors, or their successors, which
properties were located in Japan. Each issue was also guaranteed
by the Japanese Government.
According to advice just received by us from The Japan
Electric Generation and Transmission Company Limited (a successor
obligor on such Bonds) through The National City Bank of New York,
a directive was issued on August 13, 1946 by the office of the Su-
preme Allied Commander in Japan designating for removal for repa-
rations a steam power plant covered by the mortgage securing the
Daido 6-1/2% Bonds. The plant, which was one of a number so des-
ignated, is the so-called Azikawa plant (capacity 16,000 Kw.) lo-
cated at Osaka.
The directive apparently did not indicate what, if any,
provision was being made for the protection of the interests of
American bondholders in plants so designated or whether such in-
terests were considered.
The following brief history of each of the Dollar Bond
issues in question will indicate the extent and character of the
interests involved. Such outline is based on our records as pay-
ing agent in so far as it relates to the period prior to the mar
and is based as to the war period on the advices received by us
referred to above.

=== Page 399 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
Dillon, Read&Co.
Nassau & Cedar Streets
NeiYork 5
The Great Consolidated
Electric Power Company, Limited
The 7% Dollar Bonds due August 1, 1944 and the 6-1/2%
Doller Bonds due July 1, 1950 of such company were initially is-
sued under indentures of mortgege dated August 1, 1924 and July 1,
1925, respectively. The principal amount of the 7% Dollar Bonds
initially issued was $15,000,000 and the principal amount of the
6-1/2% Dollar Bonds initially issued was $13,500,000. In each
case the trustee was The Industrial Bank of Japan, Limited.
Sinking fund and interest payments were made up to De-
cember, 194l upon such Bond issues, since which time there have
been continuing defaults. According to our records there were
outstanding at the time of the first default $2,397,000 of the 7%
Dollar Bonds and $2,337,000 of the 6-1/2% Dollar Bonds, such prin-
cipal amounts in each case including Bonds held in Japan.
In the year 1938, The Japan Electric Generation and
Transmission Company Limited was organized pursuant to a special
law enacted April 6, 1938. In April, 1939 such company acquired
all of the properties of The Great Consolidated Electric Power Com-
pany, Limited and assumed the latter's obligations for the payment
of principal, interest and sinking fund on the outstanding 7% and
6-1/2% Dollar Bonds. At the same time and in connection with such
transaction, payment of the principal and interest on such Bonds
was unconditionally guaranteed by the Japanese Government. This
was the situation which existed at the time war was declared.
We are informed that during the war, namely on March 15,
194s, the Japanese Government promulgated a "Law Relating to the
Treatment of the Foreign Bonds*. Pursuant to such law, all of the
Japanese held 7% and 6-1/2% Dollar Bonds were converted into Bonds
payable in Japanese currency with the consent of the holders; such
Bonds as were held outside Japan were assumed as to principal and in-
terest by the Japanese Government; mortgages securing the Bonds so
assumed were purported to be discharged and rendered null and void;
and on May 15, 1944 such mortgages and all records with respect there-
to were delivered to the Bank of Japan.

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.99)
Dillon, Read &Co.
Nassau &: Cedar Streets
NevYork 5
We are informed that of the 7% Doller Bonds, $1, 351,000
principal amount was assumed by the Japanese Government (of which
$5,000 principal amount has subsequently been paid by such Govern-
ment) and $1,046,000 principal amount was cancelled or converted
by the Government under the law referred to above.
We are informed that of the 6-1/2% Dollar Bonds, $756,500
principal amount was assumed by the Japanese Government (of which
$2,000 principal amount has subsequently reen paid by such Govern-
ment) and $1,580,500 principal amount was converted by the Govern-
ment under the law referred to above.
Shinyetsu Electric Power Company
The 6-1/2% Dollar Bonds of such company were initially
issued in the principal amount of $7,650,000 under an indenture
dated December 1, 1927 from the issuer to The Mitsui Trust Company,
Limited, trustee.
At the time of the first default under such Bonds in De-
cember, 1941 there were $4,194,000 of such Bonds outstanding, which
Bonds had been assumed by The Japan Electric Generation and Trans-
mission Company Limited and guaranteed by the Japanese Government
in the same manner as the Great Consolidated Electric Power Company
Bonds.
It is our understanding that pursuant to the Japanese war
time enactment discussed above the mortgage securing such Bonds was
also purported to be cancelled in 1943, $1,265,000 of such Bonds was
assumed by the Japanese Government (of which $8,O00 principal amount
has subsequently been paid by such Government) and $2,929,000 princi-
pal amount was cancelled or converted by the Government under the law
referred to above.
It is assumed that all or substantially all of the princi-
pal amount of the Japanese issues referred to above which were not
cancelled, converted or paid are held by British subjects and American

=== Page 401 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Dillon, Read & Co.
Nassau &. Cedar Strcets
NewYork 5
citisens and that such persons need not accord recognition to
the enactment of the Japanese Government which purported to de
prive them of their security.
We are informed that interest on all of the issues of
Dollar Bonds referred to above was deposited in Japanese funds
with the respective trustees for the period up to the middle of
the year 1943, such funds to have been held in blocked accounts
by the trustees. We have not been advised as to whether such
funds are presently held by the trustees in Japan or whether they
are available or are to be made available to the American bond-
holders.
It is respectfully submitted that the foregoing summary
indicates that Americen bondholders have a direct interest in prop-
erties which at the time war was declared were subject to the mort-
gages securing such Bonds and that they also have a direct interest
in the disposition of any of such properties whether by wy of rep-
arations or otherwise. Although the names and addresses of such
bondholders are not known to us and although we cannot purport to
act as agent for such holders, we heve received numerous requests
for information as to what action is being taken to protect their
interests. We will appreciate any information which you can fur-
nish to us in this connection.
In our capacity as Paying Agent for the other issues of
Dollar Bonds listed in Schedule A attached hereto, we have also re-
ceived similar inquiries. Any information which you may be able
to furnish to us with respect to steps being taken to protect the
interests of American holders of such Dollar Bonds will also be
appreciated.
Yours respectfully,

DEPARTMENT OF
His Majesty's Embassy understands that the Custodian
of Alien Property has in his possession funds deposited in 1941
with the New York Agency of the Yokohama Specie Bank Limited for
service of the following Japanese bonds:-
1. Imperial Japanese Government External Loan of 1924
62 percent Bonds due February 1, 1954
2. Imperial Japanese Government External Loan of 1930
52 percent Bonds due May 1, 1965.
30
894.51/BONDHOLDERS/6-3047
3. City of Yokohama 6 percent Bonds due December 1,1961.
4. City of Tokyo 52 percent Bonds due October 1, 1961.
5. Taiwan Electric Power Company, Limited 5t percent
Bonds due July 1, 1971.
2.
His Majesty's Embassy further understands thet United
States bond holders were informed that they might file claims
against these funds with the Office of Alien Property, Department
of Justice before June 1, 1947. His Majesty's Embassy understands
that these dollar funds were intended for the servicing of all the
bonds in the above series including those held by British holders.
3.
His Majesty's Embassy suggests therefore that the dollar
sums now in the possession of the Custodien of Alien Property should
be considered as due to all the bondholders whose claims to payment
should rank equally and asks the Department of State to give
consideration to permitting British bondholders an opportunity of
filing claim against these funds even though the date of June 1,
1947 has now passed.
